# The Nine Gems of Ramal <term>Ramal: A form of divination or "geomancy" that uses patterns of dots and lines to predict future events. Though it has Persian roots, it became a significant branch of Indian astrology.</term>

---

<note>The original title page features an octagonal decorative emblem containing the words "Ramal Navaratna," surrounded by stylized diamond-cut gems representing the "Nine Gems" of the title.</note>

->Khemraj Shrikrishnadas Publishing,<-
->Bombay<- <note>original: "बम्बई" (Bambai). Now modern-day Mumbai.</note>

# The Nine Gems of <term>Ramal<note>Ramal is a traditional system of geomancy or divination, often involving patterns of dots or symbols, which originated in the Middle East and was later integrated into Indian astrological practice.</note></term>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the Table of Contents of the previous page to the formal opening of the *Ramal Navaratna* (The Nine Gems of Geomancy). It includes the traditional Sanskrit invocation to Lord Ganesha, an introduction by the commentator Mahidhar, and details regarding his patronage under King Balwant Singh of Varanasi and the publisher Seth Khemraj.</meta>

->Salutations to the Holy Ganesha.<-

# The Nine Gems of Geomancy
## Including a commentary in the vernacular language.

---

By gaining whose grace all the gods reside in their own abodes, free from the fear of enemies; upon whom the throngs of sages meditate—I perpetually bow to the <term>Large-Bellied One<note>original: *lambodaram*; a common epithet for Lord Ganesha, the elephant-headed deity</note></term>, who is the cause of the destruction of all obstacles. (1)

Having bowed to the Lord of Fortune <note>original: *shrinatham*; referring either to Lord Vishnu or the author's own spiritual teacher</note> whose lotus-feet spread auspiciousness and splendor throughout the world, and to Lord Ganesha—I, Mahidhar, a virtuous man and resident of Tehri Garhwal, am composing the vernacular commentary of the *Ramal Navaratna* <note>The Nine Gems of Geomancy</note> for the pleasure of the world, following the command of Seth Khemraj.

**Commentary**—To ensure the completion of the work without hindrance, the author bows to his chosen deity. By whose grace all the gods, having cast aside the fear of enemies such as demons, remain established in their respective positions, offices, or worlds; and upon whom the sages meditate for the perfection of their penance—to that Ganesha, I, the author, bow repeatedly to destroy any obstacles in the composition of this book. (1)

From the minister Avasan Singh, who served Balwant Singh, the ruler of Varanasi and head of the Gautama lineage, I obtained my livelihood and supreme primal happiness; I, of the Sanadhya <note>A sub-caste of Brahmins</note> community, composed this *Ramal Navaratna* while residing in <term>Avantika<note>Modern-day Ujjain, a city historically significant for astronomy and divination</note></term>. (2)

**Commentary**—From Avasan Singh, the minister of the King of Kashi, Balwant Singh (who is preeminent in the lineage of the sage Gautama), the one who has obtained his livelihood...

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the "Ramal Navaratna," opening with an invocation to Ganesha and a biographical introduction by the commentator, Mahidhar. He identifies his patron as the minister of King Balwant Singh of Varanasi and notes that the work was published under the authority of Seth Khemraj.</summary>
<keywords>Ganesha, Ramal Navaratna, geomancy, Mahidhar, Tehri Garhwal, Balwant Singh, Varanasi, Khemraj Shrikrishnadass, Sanskrit invocation, Hindi commentary</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Ramal Navaratna" (The Nine Gems of Geomancy). The previous page established the lineage and patronage of the commentator, Mahidhar. Here, the focus shifts to the original author, Paramasukha, and the editor/reviser, Ranglal, before listing the specific contents of the nine chapters (the "Gems").</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>[ The Nine Gems of Geomancy- ]</header>

Thus, the scholar named Paramasukha, born of the <term>Sanadhya<note>A prominent Brahmin sub-caste</note></term> lineage and residing in the land of <term>Avanti<note>The ancient name for the region around Ujjain in modern-day Madhya Pradesh, traditionally a center for astronomical and astrological learning</note></term>, composed this geomancy text titled *The Nine Gems*. (2)

> Because the original work was entirely impure <note>original: *ashuddham*; meaning it contained errors in transcription or calculation</note> in every respect, the minds of learned people found no joy in it. Therefore, having been requested by scholars to purify it, a man named Ranglal now clarifies this [version of] the *Nine Gems*. <note>original: "tadastyashuddham nitaram samantannatravidvajjancitta modah..."</note> (3)

**Commentary**—That original text was exceedingly incorrect in all places, so much so that the minds of scholars were not satisfied with it. Consequently, at this time, having been requested by the community of learned men to correct it, the individual named Ranglal is bringing forth this version of the *Nine Gems*. (3)

->Now, the names and sequence of the "Nine Gems" thus created are stated.<-

> [The chapters are] Technical Terms, Strengths and Weaknesses, the Tools for Inquiry, answering Unvoiced Questions, Time Limits, Items Held in the Fist, and the Identification of Thieves. (4) These nine gems of the Year’s Results are delightful and create wonder in the hearts of virtuous people on this earth. <note>original: "samjna-balabala-dalopakaranamuka-prashnottara..."</note> (5)

**Commentary**—Now the author describes the names and order of the "Nine Gems" (here, the chapters are designated as "Gems"):
1. The first is the Gem of **Technical Terms** <term>samjna<note>Basic definitions, symbols, and foundational rules of geomancy</note></term>.
2. The second is **Strengths and Weaknesses** <term>balabala<note>Analyzing the relative power and influence of the geomantic figures</note></term>.
3. The third is the **Tools for Inquiry** <term>prashnopakarana<note>Explaining the physical means and preparatory methods of performing a reading</note></term>, which describes the instruments of the question.
4. The fourth is **Proclaiming the Inquiry**.
5. The fifth is **Time Limits** <term>avadhi<note>Determining the specific duration or timeframe for when a prediction will manifest</note></term>.
6. The sixth is **Items Held in the Fist** <term>mushtigata-dravya<note>A technique to identify a hidden object held in someone's hand</note></term>.
7. The seventh is **Unvoiced Questions** <term>muka-prashna<note>The art of answering a question that the seeker has thought of but not spoken aloud</note></term>.
8. The eighth is **Identifying the Thief**.
9. The ninth is **The Yearly Chart** <term>varshapatra<note>Constructing an annual forecast or almanac</note></term>.

Through these nine gems, this book is charming and produces a sense of wonder in the minds of virtuous people within this world. (4-5)

> Having taken this collection of "Nine Gems"—woven with the spreading tendrils of the vine of his own intellect <term>buddhi-vratati<note>A poetic metaphor comparing intelligence to a growing, climbing vine</note></term>—may the scholar, who has attained great majesty and stainless fame, shine like a royal umbrella constantly in the assemblies of wise kings. <note>original: "krtvasvakanthagamamumnavaratnasangham..."</note> (6)

**Commentary**—By taking this collection of Nine Gems, which has been expanded through the vine of his intellect...

<header>First Ramal 1. ]</header>

By stringing [this knowledge] together in a thread and wearing it around one’s neck—meaning, by contemplating it through the intellect and keeping it at the tip of one's tongue—a scholar attains great majesty. Bearing the parasol of pure fame, such a wise person shines repeatedly in the royal assembly. (6)

> This "Nine Gems" is to be kept extremely secret; it must never be given to wicked people. One should not speak of this highly confidential work to those who insult their teachers, Brahmins, or the gods, nor to those who are depraved. <note>original: "navaratnamahotigopaniyam nahi deyam kuhachit su durjanebhyah..."</note> (7)

**Commentary**—This *Nine Gems* is to be kept very hidden, or rather, it should be carefully protected. It must never be given to the wicked. Furthermore, one should not speak of this extremely secret *Nine Gems* to those who speak ill of their <term>Guru<note>A teacher or spiritual guide</note></term>, the <term>Brahmins<note>The priestly and scholarly class in Hindu society</note></term>, or the deities, nor to those of corrupted intellect, atheists, or those who have abandoned their religious duties and traditional actions. (7)

->Since the dice are of primary importance in this science, the method for constructing the dice is stated first.<-

> When the tropical Sun enters Aries, forge beautiful and smooth [dice]. Mark four dots on the top, two dots on the bottom, and three on each side. From iron rods, let these eight dice be made and contemplated as the four elements. <note>original: "vasvaksangurusayane sulalitam snigdhamravau meshage..."</note> (8)

**Commentary**—Now, because the dice are paramount in this science of <term>Ramal<note>A form of divination, related to Western geomancy, that uses dice or random markings to produce figures for interpretation</note></term>, the method for making the dice is described first. On the day the <term>Sayana<note>The "Tropical" zodiac system, which calculates positions based on the seasons and equinoxes rather than the fixed stars</note></term> Sun enters the sign of Aries—specifically, the day in the month of Chaitra when the Sun’s position in <term>Signs, Degrees, Minutes, and Seconds<note>original: *Ra. An. Ka. Vi.* (Rashi, Ansha, Kala, Vikala)</note></term> results in day and night being of equal length—one should make the dice. Some texts prescribe using an <term>eight-metal alloy<note>original: *ashtadhatu*; a traditional and auspicious mixture of eight metals including gold, silver, copper, and others</note></term>. One should make eight pieces that are four-sided, heavy, attractive, and smooth. Mark 4 dots on the upper side, 2 dots on the bottom side, and 3 dots on each of the two side faces. These eight...

### Now, the time for casting the dice is stated.

> One should cast the pair of dice during the lunar dates of the third, fifth, eighth, thirteenth, fourteenth, eighteenth, twenty-first, twenty-fourth, sixteenth, and twenty-fifth. A wise practitioner should cast them on Tuesday, Friday, or Saturday, after the first four and a half hours of the day has passed, or during the night. <note>original: "chandrodayadahanivahni sharashravishveshakradhritiprakriti..."</note> (9)

**Commentary**—First, the time for throwing the dice is explained. According to the <term>Hijri calendar<note>The Islamic lunar calendar, used here because Ramal geomancy has roots in Arabic traditions.</note></term> (the Muslim dating system), the wise practitioner should cast the pair of dice on the following lunar dates: 3, 5, 8, 13, 14, 18, 21, 24, 16, and 25. Additionally, the casting should be done on Tuesday, Friday, or Saturday, starting from one and a half <term>Pahars<note>A traditional unit of time; one and a half pahars is approximately 4.5 hours after sunrise.</note></term> into the day, and also during the night. (9)

### Now, the method for casting the dice.

> Having bathed in the morning and dressed in pure garments, the wise practitioner should meditate upon their chosen deity and remember their Guru. Placing the Lahan figure on the pair of dice first and chanting the mantra, they should cast them seven times. <note>original: "pratah snatva shuddhavastravritojnah sveshtam dhyatva..."</note> (10)

**Commentary**—Now, the method for casting the dice is explained. In the morning, after bathing and wearing clean clothes, the scholar should meditate upon their <term>Ishta-devata<note>A personal deity or "cherished divinity" chosen for private worship.</note></term> and remember the lotus feet of their <term>Guru<note>Spiritual teacher</note></term>. <page-num>5</page-num>
<header>First Column 1. ]</header>

Among the dice-casters, first one should form the *Udyan* figure <gloss>the geomantic symbol for "threshold" or "entry," represented by Ξ</gloss> in their hand. After reciting the mantra seven times, one should throw the dice onto the dice-board. (10)

-> Now, the mantra to be recited is stated: <-

> **Om! Salutations to the Goddess Kushmandini, the accomplisher of all tasks, the illuminator of all omens! Come, come! Hasten, hasten! Grant the boon! Hiliri, O Matangini! Speak the truth, speak the truth! Svaha!**
<note>Kushmandini and Matangini are powerful aspects of the Divine Mother in the Tantric tradition, invoked here to ensure the accuracy of the divination.</note>

-> This is the mantra for consecrating the dice. <-

-> Now, the method of the **Expansion** is stated. <-

Once the pair of dice has fallen, one should write the auspicious marks based on the "moon-faced" points situated in front. From the single points, draw a horizontal line; repeat this procedure from the top down. (11) Having formed the first part, one should create four primary figures. Following a transverse order, from those four, generate the fifth through the eighth... (12) Where single lines and double points meet, join them according to their own nature. In this way, a "zero" <note>original: 'shunya', referring to the double-dot symbol</note> is formed; perform this union throughout the process. (13) The "half" of the first and second figures produces the tenth house; the third and fourth likewise. From the fifth, the twelfth is obtained; the seventh and eighth produce the eleventh. (14) By the union of the ninth and tenth, the thirteenth is born; from the eleventh and twelfth, the fourteenth appears. By the union of those two, the fifteenth is born, and the sixteenth is then generated by the first and fifteenth. Thus was the sixteen-petaled <term>Prastara<note>The 'expansion' or complete layout of the 16 geomantic houses.</note></term> described by the <term>Yavanas<note>Literally "Ionians" or Greeks; in this context, it refers to the Perso-Arabic masters from whom the science of Ramal originated.</note></term> in ancient times. (15)

**Commentary**—Now the method for creating the <term>Prastara</term> or <term>Zairaja<note>Derived from the Arabic *Zairaja*, an astronomical or divinatory chart used to calculate answers.</note></term> is explained. When the dice have been thrown onto the board, keep the pair aligned together. Then, write the upper <term>Bindu<note>A 'point' or dot. In this system, a single dot indicates an active force, while two dots or a line indicate stability.</note></term> of the first segment on your right-hand side. Then, write the one below it. In this same manner, record the points from all eight segments of the dice. The specific rule here is that a single point remains a point, but two points are written as a single horizontal line...

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>First Jewel 1.]</header>

### ->Now, the rules for the numbers of the house segments are stated.<-

Fire, Air, Water, and Earth elements proceed in order from top to bottom.
By their variations and also by their reverse order, there are exactly sixteen house numbers. ॥ 16 ॥

**Commentary** — Now the rules for the number of segments in the sixteen houses <note>In geomancy, a "house" refers to a specific position in a divinatory chart, similar to astrology.</note> are described. The markers above the segments represent the **Fire element** <term>Agni-tattva</term>, second the **Air element** <term>Vayu-tattva</term>, third the **Water element** <term>Jala-tattva</term>, and fourth the **Earth element** <term>Prithvi-tattva</term>. In Persian <note>original: "Parshi"</note>, these are also called *Atashi* (Fire), *Khaki* (Earth), *Abi* (Water), and *Badi* (Air). It is stated that they follow a top-to-bottom order, but sometimes they are counted in reverse, which results in sixteen variations, and sometimes two variations. ॥ 16 ॥

### ->Now, the names of the segments and the sequence for omens.<-

Lahyan, Qabzul Dakhil, Qabzul Kharij, Jama'at, Farha, 
Uqla, Ankish, Humra, and for the purpose of omens, next are Bayaz, 
Nusratul Kharij, Nusratul Dakhil, Atabul Kharij, 
Naqi, Atabul Dakhil, Ijtima, and Tariq; these are 
declared in the sequential order of their causes. ॥ 17 ॥

**Commentary** — Now the names of the segments <term>shakal</term> <gloss>geomantic figures</gloss> and the sequence for reading omens are explained. The first figure is **Lahyan** <gloss>⫶</gloss> <note>often associated with Great Fortune or "The Harvest"</note>, the second is **Qabzul Dakhil** <gloss>•—•—</gloss> <note>Acquisition Inward</note>, the third is **Qabzul Kharij** <gloss>—•—•</gloss> <note>Acquisition Outward</note>, the fourth is **Jama'at** <gloss>≡</gloss> <note>The Congregation</note>, the fifth is **Farha** <gloss>— — • •</gloss> <note>Joy</note>, the sixth is **Uqla** <gloss>• • — —</gloss> <note>The Knot</note>, the seventh is **Ankish** <gloss>• — — •</gloss> <note>The Reversed/Hook</note>, the eighth is **Humra** <gloss>— • • —</gloss> <note>Redness</note>, the ninth is **Bayaz** <gloss>≡ •</gloss> <note>Whiteness</note>, the tenth is **Nusratul Kharij** <gloss>• ≡</gloss> <note>Victory Outward</note>, the eleventh is **Nusratul Dakhil** <gloss>≡ •</gloss> <note>Victory Inward</note>, the twelfth is **Atabul Kharij** <gloss>— • • •</gloss> <note>Destruction Outward</note>, the thirteenth is **Naqi** <gloss>• • • —</gloss> <note>Purity</note>, the fourteenth is **Atabul Dakhil** <gloss>• • • —</gloss> <note>Destruction Inward</note>, the fifteenth is **Ijtima** <gloss>≡</gloss> <note>The Conjunction</note>, and the sixteenth is **Tariq** <gloss>⫶</gloss> <note>The Path</note>. These are the sixteen figures. This is the primary sequence used for omens; other sequences will be described in the upcoming circular charts. ॥ 17 ॥

**Lahya** <gloss>≡</gloss> is formed of points and lines... and **Ankish** <gloss>≡</gloss> is its reverse.
**Qabzul Dakhil** consists of two pairs of lines <gloss>— — • •</gloss> and also...

<page-num>८</page-num>
<header>The Nine Jewels of Ramal</header>

The inverse of that is **Kharij** <note>original: "Kharij" means Outward; pattern: · · - -</note>; the pair of lines is **Jama'at** <note>original: "Jama'at" means Congregation; pattern: - - - -</note>. ॥ १८ ॥
The opposite is **Tariq** <note>original: "Tariq" means The Path; pattern: · · · ·</note>; the pattern with a point and no lines is **Farah** <note>original: "Farah" means Joy; pattern: - · · ·</note>; its inverse is **Naqi** <note>original: "Naqi" means Pure; pattern: · · · -</note>. Between the lines of **Uqla** <note>original: "Uqla" means The Knot; pattern: · - - ·</note> sits the inverse, **Ijtima** <note>original: "Ijtima" means Conjunction; pattern: - · · -</note>. **Humra** is the line-point-line-line <note>pattern: - · - -</note>. ॥ १९ ॥
The opposite of that is **Bayaz** <note>original: "Bayaz" means White; pattern: - - · -</note>. Two lines and two lines <note>Wait, the text describes specific patterns for Nusrat</note> is **Nusratul Kharij** <note>pattern: · · - -</note>; **Nusratul Dakhil** <note>pattern: - - · ·</note> is its high inverse. **Atabul Kharij** <note>original: "Ataba" means Threshold; pattern: · · · -</note> is formed from the final line. ॥ २० ॥
**Atabul Dakhil** <note>pattern: - · · ·</note> is considered the inverse of this. These forms I have declared were spoken by the wise men of old. ॥ २१ ॥

### Commentary
A single point <term>shunya</term> above and three lines <term>rekha</term> below <note>pattern: · - - -</note> is the figure **Lahyan** <gloss>The Beard</gloss>. Its inverse <note>pattern: - - - ·</note> is **Ankish** <gloss>The Hook</gloss>. A line, a point, a line, and a point <note>pattern: - · - ·</note> is **Qabzul Dakhil** <gloss>Internal Seizure</gloss>. Its inverse <note>pattern: · - · -</note> is **Qabzul Kharij** <gloss>External Seizure</gloss>. A group of four lines <note>pattern: - - - -</note> is **Jama'at** <gloss>The Community</gloss>. Four points <note>pattern: · · · ·</note> is **Tariq** <gloss>The Path</gloss>. Two points, a line, and a point <note>pattern: · · - ·</note> is **Farah** <gloss>Joy</gloss>. Its inverse <note>pattern: · - · ·</note> is **Naqi** <gloss>Pure</gloss>. A point between two lines <note>The text specifies the configuration for the "Knot"</note> is **Uqla** <gloss>The Knot; pattern: · - - ·</gloss>. Its inverse is **Ijtima** <gloss>The Meeting; pattern: - · · -</gloss>. A line, a point, and two lines is **Humra** <gloss>Red; pattern: - · - -</gloss>. Its inverse is **Bayaz** <gloss>White; pattern: - - · -</gloss>. Two points and two lines is **Nusratul Kharij** <gloss>Outward Victory; pattern: · · - -</gloss>. Its inverse is **Nusratul Dakhil** <gloss>Inward Victory; pattern: - - · ·</gloss>. Three points and one line is **Atabul Kharij** <gloss>The Outer Threshold; pattern: · · · -</gloss>. The inverse is **Atabul Dakhil** <gloss>The Inner Threshold; pattern: - · · ·</gloss>. In this manner, I have described the forms of the 16 figures <term>shakal</term> as told by the ancient scholars. ॥ १८-२१ ॥

->### Now, the technical designations such as "Outward" for the figures.<-

When the top and bottom positions of a figure consist of a point and a line, it is called **Kharij** <gloss>Outward</gloss>. From the inverse of that, it becomes **Dakhil** <gloss>Inward</gloss>. When both the top and bottom are joined by lines, it is called **Savit** <gloss>Stable</gloss>. From the inverse of that <gloss>when both are points</gloss>, it becomes **Munkalib** <gloss>Moving or Converted</gloss>. ॥ २२ ॥

<page-num>१</page-num>
<header>Chapter One.]</header>

**Commentary**—Those figures which have dots <term>bindu</term> at the top and lines <term>rekha</term> below are:
<pre>
 .   .   .   . 
 -   -   .   . 
 -   .   -   . 
 -   -   -   - 
</pre>
**Kharij** <gloss>Outward</gloss>. Those with lines at the top and dots below are:
<pre>
 -   -   -   - 
 .   .   .   - 
 .   -   .   . 
 .   .   .   . 
</pre>
**Dakhil** <gloss>Inward</gloss>. Those with lines at both the top and bottom are:
<pre>
 -   -   -   - 
 -   .   -   . 
 -   .   .   - 
 -   -   -   - 
</pre>
**Sabit** <gloss>Stable or Fixed</gloss>. Those with dots at both the top and bottom are:
<pre>
 .   .   .   . 
 -   .   .   - 
 -   .   -   . 
 .   .   .   . 
</pre>
known as **Munkleev** <gloss>Changeable or Volatile</gloss>. ॥ २२ ॥

*Lahyan, Atave-kharij, Nusrat-kharij, and Kanjul-kharij*
*Are masculine and outward-moving; they are strong in the day*
*And are auspicious and inauspicious in that specific order. ॥ २३ ॥*
*Kanjul-dakhil, Ankeesh, Atave-dakhil, and Nusrat-dakhil*
*Are feminine and inward-moving; they are strong at night.*
*Except for Ankeesh, the rest are of a lucky nature. ॥ २४ ॥*
*Jamaat, Ijtima, Humra, Bayaz, and Sabit*
*Are neuter; they are strong at twilight and have moderate power,*
*Alternating between lucky and unlucky in sequence. ॥ २५ ॥*
*Humra is considered both masculine and neuter,*
*While the opposite is true for Bayaz, being feminine and neuter. ॥ २६ ॥*
*Naqi, Tariq, Farha, and Ukla are the Changeable figures;*
*Strong at twilight, they are neither lucky nor unlucky but neutral.*
*Among these, Farha is masculine, while the others are feminine. ॥ २७ ॥*

**Commentary**—The figures **Lahyan** <gloss>The Beard</gloss>, **Atave-kharij** <gloss>The Outer Threshold</gloss>, **Nusrat-kharij** <gloss>Outer Victory</gloss>, and **Kanjul-kharij** <gloss>Outer Treasure</gloss> are designated as masculine. They are categorized as **Kharij** <note>original: "kharij" means exiting or moving away</note>, meaning they are active during the day. In the order they are listed, one is auspicious <term>shubh</term> and the next is inauspicious <term>ashubh</term>. ॥ २३ ॥

**Kanjul-dakhil** <gloss>Inner Treasure</gloss>, **Ankeesh** <gloss>The Hook</gloss>, **Atave-dakhil** <gloss>The Inner Threshold</gloss>, and **Nusrat-dakhil** <gloss>Inner Victory</gloss> are designated as feminine. They gain strength during the night. Except for **Ankeesh**, the other three are considered auspicious. ॥ २४ ॥

The figures **Jamaat** <gloss>The Congregation</gloss>, **Ijtima** <gloss>The Conjunction</gloss>, **Humra** <gloss>The Red</gloss>, **Bayaz** <gloss>The White</gloss>, and **Sabit** <gloss>The Stable</gloss> are neuter. They are most powerful during the twilight hours <note>original: "sandhya" refers to the transition between day and night</note>. Their strength is moderate, and they alternate between being lucky and unlucky. ॥ २५ ॥

**Humra** is considered both masculine and neuter; its opposite is **Bayaz**, which is feminine and neuter. ॥ २६ ॥

**Naqi** <gloss>The Pure</gloss>, **Tariq** <gloss>The Path</gloss>, **Farha** <gloss>Joy</gloss>, and **Ukla** <gloss>The Knot</gloss> are known as **Munkleev** <note>original: "Munkleev" is a Sanskritization of the Arabic "Munqalib," meaning those that turn or change</note>. They are strong at twilight and are neither lucky nor unlucky, but rather neutral or middling. Among these, **Farha** is masculine, while the others are feminine in nature. ॥ २७ ॥

<summary>This page classifies the sixteen geomantic figures into four movement categories (Outward, Inward, Stable, and Changeable) and assigns them gender, time-based strength, and lucky/unlucky attributes.</summary>
<keywords>Ramal Shastra, Kharij, Dakhil, Sabit, Munkleev, Lahyan, Humra, Bayaz, masculine and feminine figures, geomancy, divination</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical classification of the sixteen geomantic figures. While the previous page categorized them by their visual structure (dots and lines) and basic qualities (Inward, Outward, Stable, Volatile), this page assigns them to the four elements, cardinal directions, colors, zodiac signs, and planetary rulers. This layering of correspondences allows the practitioner to interpret specific details like the physical appearance of a person or the direction of a lost object.</meta>

<page-num>१०</page-num>
<header>The Nine Jewels of Ramal</header>

*Lahyan, Atve-kharij, Nusrat-kharij, and Kabbul-kharij* <note>These are the Sanskrit transliterations of the Arabic figures: Al-Lihyan, Utbat al-Kharij, Nasrat al-Kharij, and Qabd al-Kharij.</note> *are of the Fire element. They are powerful in the East and are yellow in color. ॥ २८ ॥*

*Humra, Farha, Ijtima, and Atve-dakhil belong to the element of Air. They are powerful in the West and are red in color. ॥ २९ ॥*

*Bayaz, Naqi, Nusrat-dakhil, and the one named Tariq are of the Water element. They possess strength in the North and are white in color. ॥ ३० ॥*

*Kabbul-dakhil, Ankeesh, Ukba, and Jamaat are of the Earth element. They are powerful in the South and are dark or black in color. ॥ ३१ ॥*

**Commentary**—The figures **Lahyan**, **Atve-kharij**, **Nusrat-kharij**, and **Kabbul-kharij** represent the Fire element <term>Agni-tattva</term>; they are strong in the Eastern direction and are yellow. ॥ २८ ॥ **Humra**, **Farha**, **Ijtima**, and **Atve-dakhil** represent the Air element <term>Vayu-tattva</term>; they are strong in the Western direction and are red. ॥ २९ ॥ **Bayaz**, **Naqi**, **Nusrat-dakhil**, and **Tariq** represent the Water element <term>Jala-tattva</term>; they are strong in the Northern direction and are white. ॥ ३० ॥ **Kabbul-dakhil**, **Ankeesh**, **Ukba**, and **Jamaat** represent the Earth element <term>Prithvi-tattva</term>; they are strong in the Southern direction and are dark-colored. ॥ ३१ ॥

*Nusrat-dakhil and Lahyan correspond to the zodiac signs of Sagittarius and Pisces, and their deity is Jupiter. Nusrat-kharij and Kabbul-dakhil are associated with the color green.* <note>original: "harirou" — this term often refers to the planet Mercury in some Ramal traditions, or simply the color green.</note> *॥ ३२ ॥*

*Jamaat and Ijtima correspond to the signs of Gemini and Virgo, and are known to be ruled by Mercury. Tariq and Bayaz correspond to the sign of Cancer and have the Moon as their presiding deity. ॥ ३३ ॥*

*Farha and Atve-dakhil correspond to the signs of Libra and Taurus and are ruled by Venus. Humra and Naqi are known to be of Mars, corresponding to the signs of Aries and Scorpio. ॥ ३४ ॥*

*Ukba and Ankeesh correspond to the signs of Capricorn and Aquarius and are ruled by Saturn. Kabbul-kharij and Atve-kharij are associated with the lunar nodes Rahu and Ketu.* <note>The text uses "Dhatenakau", a specialized term for the Rahu/Ketu axis in this context.</note> *॥ ३५ ॥*

*Figures categorized as "Munkleev" are considered Moveable <gloss>Chara</gloss>; those called "Sabit" are considered Fixed <gloss>Sthira</gloss>. There are also those of a Dual nature <gloss>Dwishabhava</gloss>, which can be either Outward-moving or Inward-moving. ॥ ३६ ॥*

<summary>This page defines the elemental, directional, and astrological correspondences for the sixteen geomantic figures, establishing a system where each figure is linked to a specific planet, zodiac sign, and color.</summary>
<keywords>elements, directions, zodiac signs, planetary rulers, Lahyan, Humra, Bayaz, Kabbul, Agni-tattva, Vayu-tattva, Jala-tattva, Prithvi-tattva</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the systematic classification of geomantic figures, moving from their elemental and directional properties to their planetary rulers and specific zodiacal associations. It then begins a detailed section on the physical and temperamental "portraits" of each figure, used to describe people or outcomes in a divination.</meta>

<page-num>११</page-num>
<header>The First Chapter 1.] ( 11 )</header>

**Commentary**—**Nusrat-dakhil** <note>original: "nusrud-dakhil", Arabic: Nasrat al-Dakhil, meaning "Inward Victory"</note> and **Lahyan** belong to the 9th and 12th zodiac signs <note>Sagittarius and Pisces</note>; **Jupiter** is their ruler. **Nusrat-kharij** <note>Arabic: Nasrat al-Kharij, meaning "Outward Victory"</note> and **Kabbul-dakhil** <note>OCR reads "Kanjul-dakhil", referring to Qabd al-Dakhil, "Inward Seizure"</note> belong to the 5th sign <note>Leo</note>, and the **Sun** is their ruler. ॥ ३२ ॥

**Jamaat** and **Ijtima** belong to the 3rd and 6th signs <note>Gemini and Virgo</note>; **Mercury** is their ruler. **Tariq** and **Bayaz** belong to the 4th sign <note>Cancer</note>; the **Moon** is their ruler. ॥ ३३ ॥

**Farha** and **Atve-dakhil** belong to the 7th and 2nd signs <note>Libra and Taurus</note>; **Venus** is their ruler. **Naqi** belongs to the 1st and 8th signs <note>Aries and Scorpio</note>; **Mars** is their ruler. ॥ ३४ ॥

**Ukba**, **Ankeesh**, and **Naqi** <note>The text repeats Naqi here, though it typically refers to the figures of Saturn</note> belong to the 10th and 11th signs <note>Capricorn and Aquarius</note>; **Saturn** is their ruler. **Kabbul-kharij** <note>Arabic: Qabd al-Kharij, "Outward Seizure"</note> and **Atve-kharij** belong to the 10th and 11th signs; **Rahu** and **Ketu** <note>the North and South Nodes of the Moon</note> are their rulers. ॥ ३५ ॥

The figures <term>shakal</term> that are volatile <term>munqalib</term> are classified as "movable" <note>Sanskrit: chara</note>. Those that are stable <term>thabit</term> are "fixed" <note>Sanskrit: sthira</note>. Those that are dual-natured <term>dvisvabhava</term> possess both "outward" <term>kharij</term> and "inward" <term>dakhil</term> qualities. ॥ ३६ ॥

->**Now, the caste, color, temperament, and physical appearance of the figures.**<-

The first figure [**Lahyan**] is born of the mouth <note>of the Brahmin or priestly caste</note>, fair-complexioned, righteous in action, and speaks sweet and desirable words. He has a slender neck and beautiful dark-blue eyes. He is devoted to the study of the gods, penance, and rubies; his body is naturally fragrant. <insert>≡</insert> ॥ ३७ ॥

**Kabbul-dakhil** is of the warrior caste <term>kshatriya</term>, possesses a sweet voice, a wheat-colored complexion, and dark eyes. He is skilled in trade and craftsmanship. He frequents temples, beautiful streets, and marketplaces. He is of medium build, fond of sweet foods, and is a treasurer or lives in a place of wealth. He is wise, possesses rubies and gold, is extremely clever, and has a fragrant appearance. <insert>⚇</insert> ॥ ३८ ॥

**Kabbul-kharij** is an outsider <term>mleccha</term>, free of physical wounds, with prominent eyes, a very loud voice, and an unjust nature. His complexion is yellowish-black. He is highly treacherous, with unlucky or protruding teeth. He prefers bitter foods and his body and clothes are often dirty. He is silent and dwells in odd-numbered forests. <insert>⚇</insert> ॥ ३९ ॥

[The next figure] has a wheatish complexion, is virtuous, and appears like gold or multi-colored. He is of the servant caste <term>shudra</term>, possesses many merits, and has dark eyes and a long face...

<page-num>१२</page-num>
<header>[ Gems of Geomancy -</header>

# One who eats sweet food, is violent, a spender, honored by physicians,
# emerald-like, and resides in a house of learning—
## Jamaat ☵ ॥ ४० ॥

**Commentary**—Now the castes, colors, temperaments, physical forms, and other characteristics of the figures are described.

**Lahyan ☲** <note>original: "lahyāna", Arabic: al-Lahyāna, "The Beard"; associated with the figure Laetitia</note>: This figure represents the Brahmin <note>the priestly and scholarly caste</note>. He is fair-complexioned, a doer of righteous deeds, and possesses sweet speech. He has a throat accustomed to sweet food, blue eyes, and is a worshiper of the gods who practices penance. He is a steady worker and fond of fragrances. ॥ ३७ ॥

**Kada ☖** <note>original: "kadā", likely a corruption of "qabd", specifically Qabd al-Dākhil, "Inward Seizure"</note>: This figure represents the Kshatriya <note>the warrior and ruling caste</note>. He has sweet speech, wheat-colored skin, and dark eyes. He is clever, a merchant of goods, and knowledgeable in the arts and crafts. He resides in temples, markets, and shops. He is of medium height, enjoys sweet food, and is a master of wealth and a treasurer. He is wise, skilled in working with rubies and metals, and proficient in financial matters. He is extremely clever, fragrant, and his shape is likened to a dog. ॥ ३८ ॥

**Kanjulkharij ☶** <note>original: "kanjulkhārij", likely Qabd al-Khārij, "Outward Seizure"</note>: This figure represents the *mleccha* <note>a term for those outside the traditional Vedic social order, often foreigners or outcasts</note>. He is without scars, has deep-set eyes, and a loud voice. He is not a man of justice. His color is a yellowish-black. He is a thief, inauspicious, and has protruding teeth. He eats bitter food. His body, house, and clothes are filthy and contemptible. His nature is the Earth element mixed with stones. He represents a "half-portion" <note>referring to a partial or incomplete result</note>. ॥ ३९ ॥

**Jamaat ☵** <note>original: "jamāta", Arabic: al-Jamā‘ah, "The People" or "The Gathering"</note>: He is wheat-colored and gains strength at twilight. He is associated with gold and the art of painting. He represents the Shudra <note>the laborer or artisan caste</note> yet is virtuous. He has dark eyes, a large mouth, and eats sweet food. He can be violent and is a spender. He is respected even by the greatest of physicians. His metal is emerald-like <term>garutmat</term>, and his residence is a schoolhouse. ॥ ४० ॥

-> The one named **Farha** ☩ <note>Arabic: al-Farah, "Joy"</note> is fortunate and has a very tall body. He is fair-complexioned, with a mind inclined toward writing and humor. He has divided eyebrows, beautiful eyes, and a small waist. He is wealthy in pearls, possesses good land, and eats sweet food. ॥ ४१ ॥

The one named **Ukka** ☗ <note>original: "ukkākhya", also called "ukba", Arabic: al-‘Uqbah, "The Tail"; associated with the Dragon's Tail</note> is dark-bodied, a farmer, and extremely filthy. He has an exceedingly tall body and is hardworking. With small eyes, he lives in a house surrounded by pillars, walls, and ditches. He is malicious and skilled in the vegetable market. He has a dark soul and a nature like an arrow. He is lazy, resides in a house with a moat, has a dull intellect, is a bearer of heavy loads, and is of the lowest birth <term>antyaja</term>. ॥ ४२ ॥ <-

<page-num>१३</page-num>
<header>The First Gem 1. ] ( १३ )</header>

**Commentary—Farha** <note>original: "farahā", Arabic: al-Farah, "Joy"; associated with the figure Laetitia</note>: He has a beautiful, very tall body and a fair complexion. His disposition is inclined toward writing and joking or mimicry. His eyebrows are distinct and separated from each other. He has lovely eyes and a small stomach. He resides in a prosperous land rich in pearls and consumes sweet food. ॥ ४१ ॥

**Uhla** <note>original: "uhlā", likely a corruption of "uqla", Arabic: al-Uqla, "The Knot"; associated with the figure Conjunctio</note>: He is a farmer, extremely soiled or dirty, with a tall body and a hardworking nature. He has small eyes and is influenced by the quality of darkness <term>Tamoguna: the psychological quality of lethargy, ignorance, or darkness</term>. He dwells within city walls or prisons. He may be a thief or a merchant of green vegetables. He has a dark body, a shape like an arrow, and is prone to laziness. He resides in fortifications, is lacking in intellect, is a bearer of heavy loads, and may even be an outcast <term>Chandala: a historical term for those outside the traditional caste system, often performing tasks considered impure</term>. ॥ ४२ ॥

> The portion related to the **Outcasts** <note>original: "mlecchoṃ", referring to foreigners or those outside the Vedic social order</note>: He is extremely dark from the labor of farming, with dark eyes, a resident of villages, and a speaker of falsehoods. He has small eyes and very strong nails and teeth. He consumes acidic food and is prone to laziness. He has a long face, stays within the home, possesses a very loud and terrifying voice, and is merciless. He is like a black stone, extremely tall, with a distorted body and face, yet he is highly skilled in the work of a servant. ॥ ४३ ॥

**Humra** <note>original: "humrā", Arabic: al-Humra, "Redness"; associated with the figure Amissio</note>: He is a barber or a blacksmith. He belongs to the warrior class <note>Kshatriya</note> and is often wounded by weapons, or he may be a physician. He is of medium build with pale, small eyes. He is a creator of quarrels and is violent. He wanders in forests and has a large head. ॥ ४४ ॥

> **Bayaaj** <note>original: "bayāja", Arabic: al-Bayad, "Whiteness"; associated with the figure Albus</note>: He is white and fair-complexioned, of tall stature and noble thoughts. He is endowed with success and has a round face. He has dark eyes and speaks clearly or inquisitively. He resides in places with water, trees, and vines, and is fond of fragrances. He is rich in pearls and crystals, possesses a beautiful and delicate luster, and consumes milk and sweet foods. His heart is devoted to the worship of the gods; he is wealthy in business, joyful, and free from deceit. ॥ ४५ ॥

**Rajakiya** <note>The Royal figure</note>: He is auspicious, with long eyes and a slender body. He is endowed with the duties of the warrior class and has a fair radiance. He has a "heart of gold" and keeps a shop of gems, gold, and jewels. He eats sweets, has a melodious voice, and is an achiever of tasks...

<page-num>१४</page-num>
<header>[ In the Nine Gems of Geomancy — </header>

> **The Outward** <note>original: "khārijaḥ", likely referring to the figure Nusrat al-Kharij or Qabd al-Kharij</note> ⠅ ॥४६॥ One who is born with a white, ascetic-like face, golden and round; he is tall with a broad body and wide eyes. He is a servant of the sea <note>or lives near the Indus river/ocean</note> and wears fine clothes. ॥४७॥ With the luster of crystal and silver, he possesses a righteous gait and a pleasant fragrance. His heart is delighted by the scriptures <note>the Vedas</note>, and he follows the path of the soul ⠅ in all things. ॥४७॥ Dwelling in forests, mountains, or near deep wells and high places, he makes his home in forts. Involved in agriculture, he is pale or yellowish with a dark body and marked by scars. ॥४८॥ Having tawny eyes and hair, he is an outsider <term>Mleccha: historically used for those outside the Vedic social order or foreigners</term> and is tall. He possesses a foul odor and wears clothes made of wool; such is the nature of the **Outward** ⠅ figure. ॥४८॥

**Commentary** — **Ankish** <note>Arabic: "al-Ankis", the geomantic figure Carcer or Tristitia</note> ⠅: An outsider, of an extremely dark color, devoted to agricultural work, with dark eyes, a resident of villages, and a speaker of lies. He has small eyes, with strong nails and teeth. He eats very little, is lazy, has a large mouth, and is a homebody. He speaks with a loud voice and has a terrifying appearance. He is merciless, associated with black stone and the metal iron, has a very tall body, and a face lacking in luster. He is clever in performing assigned tasks. ॥४३॥

**Humra** <note>Arabic: "al-Hamra", "The Red"; the figure Ruber</note> ⠅: A thief, a barber, a blacksmith, or a warrior <term>Kshatriya: the ruler and warrior caste</term>. His body is free of scars, and he carries a staff in his hand. He is knowledgeable in the science of medicine. He has a balanced yet tall body of a yellow-red color and small eyes. He is quarrelsome, a taker of life, and one who frequents forests and mountains. He consumes bitter-tasting food and has a large head. ॥ ४४ ॥

**Bayaz** <note>Arabic: "al-Bayad", "The White"; the figure Albus</note> ⠅: White-complexioned, fair-skinned, and of tall stature. He acts with wisdom in his conduct and possesses spiritual attainments <term>Siddhi: supernatural powers or spiritual perfections</term>. He has a round face, dark eyes, and speaks words that are pleasing to the mind. He resides in places near water or among trees and creepers. He deals in fragrant substances, pearls, and crystals. He possesses a beautiful and attractive luster. He consumes milk and sweets and is devoted to the worship of the gods. He attains glory through trade and is endowed with happiness. ॥ ४५ ॥

**Nusrat al-Kharij** <note>Arabic: "Nusrat al-Kharij", "Outward Victory"; the figure Fortuna Major</note> ⠅: A man of the King <note>an official or royal servant</note>, auspicious, with long eyes and a tall body. He is endowed with the duties of a warrior. He has a fair complexion and carries a golden pitcher. He is a merchant of rubies, gold, and gems. He eats sweets—

<meta>This page continues the systematic classification of geomantic figures (shakals) within the Ramal system. Having previously discussed the "Outward" figure, it now details the social castes (Varnas), physical appearances, and temperamental traits of the figures known as the Auspicious Outcome, the Outward Threshold, the Pure, the Gathering, and the Path.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>[ The First Gem 1. ]</header>

...youthful, excelling in wealth, and associated with the metal bell-metal <note>a type of bronze used for bells and bowls</note>. ॥ 46 ॥ 

**The Auspicious Outcome** <insert>⠶</insert> <note>original: "Susahakhila", likely referring to *Sa'd al-Kharij* or Fortuna Major</note>: A Brahmin <term>the priestly and scholarly class</term>, white in color, an ascetic, and associated with the metal gold. He has a round, large face, a broad body, and large eyes. He is a servant of the sea <note>or lives near the coast</note> and wears fine garments. Possessing a luster like crystal or silver, he eats excellent foods, wears fine fragrances, and his mind is ever-adorned by the wisdom of the Vedas <note>ancient Hindu scriptures</note>. ॥ 47 ॥ 

**The Outward Threshold** <insert>⠋</insert> <note>original: "Atave Kharij", from the Arabic *Utbat al-Kharij*</note>: He dwells in forests, mountains, near wells, and in high places. He makes his home in forts. He is physically weakened by agricultural labor, of a yellowish-black complexion, and marked by flaws or scars. His eyelashes and hair are brown. He is an outsider <term>Mleccha: a term used for those outside the traditional Vedic social order or foreigners</term>, tall in stature, foul-smelling, and wears woolen clothes. ॥ 48 ॥

**The Pure** <insert>⠃</insert> <note>original: "Naki", from the Arabic *al-Naqi*, corresponding to the figure Albus</note>: Fair-skinned and of the Kshatriya <term>the warrior and ruling class</term> caste, with a thin body and small, bright yellow eyes. He is well-versed in the scriptures, a meat-eater, wears green garments, and has red hair. A soldier with a thick neck, he lives near dark waters or meadows. He is dependent on others, can be violent, has prominent teeth, and is a servant of the young. ॥ 49 ॥ 

**The Constrained** <note>original: "Makuntika", likely referring to *Uqlah* or Carcer</note>: Very tall with dark limbs, a handsome face with distinct eyebrows. He is a lover of fine things and dwells in simple homes. He lives where there are trees and water. With beautiful eyes, he wears wheat-colored clothes, possesses a graceful body, and is blessed with great fortune and wealth. ॥ 50 ॥ 

**The Gathering** <insert>⠿</insert> <note>original: "Jama", from the Arabic *al-Jama’a*, corresponding to the figure Populus</note>: A scribe for kings, a calculator of numbers <note>an astrologer or mathematician</note>, and a seeker of virtues. He has fine eyes and a bearded face, associated with two metals <note>or elements</note>. Adorned by various fine cloths and the study of texts, he is of the Shudra <term>the laborer or artisan class</term> caste, gentle and modest. ॥ 51 ॥ 

**The Path** <insert>⠇</insert> <note>original: "Tarikh/Tariq", from the Arabic *al-Tariq*, corresponding to the figure Via</note>: A Vaishya <term>the merchant and agricultural class</term>, with a body attracted to white or bright things. He is very wealthy, with long limbs and eyes, and resides in the palaces of kings or travels as a wanderer. He is found on paths, near water, and on mountains. He eats whatever he desires, wears beautiful clothes, and is associated with the passage of time or dates. ॥ 52 ॥

-> Thus end the descriptions of the forms, natures, and castes of the figures. <-

**Commentary** — **The Pure** <insert>⠃</insert> <note>Albus</note>: Of fair complexion and the Kshatriya caste, with a thin body and small yellow eyes. He is knowledgeable in the scriptures, eats meat, wears green clothes, has red hair, and is a warrior...

<summary>This page lists the physical and social characteristics of various geomantic figures, assigning them to the four Hindu castes (Varnas) and describing their habitats, clothing, and temperaments.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific physical and social descriptions of the geomantic figures (shakals) and begins the technical explanation of how these figures are grouped into "Mothers," "Daughters," and "Witnesses," as well as their placement in astrological houses.</meta>

<page-num>( 16 )</page-num>
<header>[ The Nine Gems of Ramal ]</header>

...thick-necked, associated with darkness, dwelling near reservoirs and at the edge of the city. He is a servant to others, a killer of living beings <note>this may suggest a hunter or butcher by trade</note>, possesses large teeth, and is a protector of children. ॥ 49 ॥

Tall-bodied with strong limbs, a beautiful face, and shapely eyebrows; he is marked by moles on distinct parts of his body. He possesses fine things, is ready for love, and is a blacksmith or one who dwells in an armory. [This is] **The Inward Threshold** <note>original: "Atve-dakhil", from the Arabic *Utbat al-Dakhil*</note>. ॥ 50 ॥

**The Gathering** <note>original: "Izzat-ma", from the Arabic *al-Ijtimā'*</note>: A royal scribe, an astrologer, a seeker of virtues, with pleasing eyes and a beard. He is associated with mountains and metals, wears multi-colored items and garments, and dwells in schools. He is of the Shudra <term>the laborer or artisan class</term> caste, has a gentle nature, and is modest. ॥ 51 ॥

**The Path** <note>original: "Tarikh", from the Arabic *al-Tariq*</note>: Of the Vaishya <term>the merchant and farmer class</term> caste, white in color, with a relaxed body and an air of greatness. Every limb has its own distinct beauty; he has large eyes and dwells in royal palaces. He is a traveler <note>original: 'char', meaning mobile or wandering</note> who moves across roads, waters, and mountains. He enjoys excellent things, wears beautiful clothes, and possesses a dual nature <term>Dvisvabhava: a term in Indian astrology for signs or figures that are mutable or hold two qualities</term>. ॥ 52 ॥

### Now, the classification of the Mothers and other groups.

> The first group is the Mothers, then the Daughters, then the third is the Nephews. The fourth of these is the Witnesses. These are the names given to the groups of four. ॥ 53 ॥ <note>These are technical terms in Ramal (Geomancy): *Ummahat* (Mothers), *Banat* (Daughters), *Mawalid* (Nephews), and *Zawa'id* (Witnesses).</note>

> The first, fourth, seventh, and tenth houses are known as the Pillars <term>Avatad: from the Arabic 'awtād', meaning stakes or pillars, corresponding to the 'Kendra' or angular houses in Indian astrology</term>. The second, fifth, eighth, and eleventh houses are known by the wise as the Succeedent houses <term>Panaphara: the Sanskrit equivalent for houses that follow the angles</term>. ॥ 54 ॥

> The third, sixth, ninth, and twelfth houses are called the Cadent houses <term>Apoklima or Jayal</term>. The collection of the first four houses [the Mothers] is also specifically designated as the root or origin. ॥ 55 ॥

Commentary—Now the classifications of the Mothers (*Ummahat*) and others are stated. The first group of four houses is called the Mothers, the second is the Daughters (*Banant*), the third is the Nephews (*Munnul-ladat*), and the fourth is the Witnesses (*Jaway-dad*). These are the names for each set of four houses.

<summary>This page concludes the descriptions of the geomantic figures "The Inward Threshold," "The Gathering," and "The Path," and introduces the technical division of the sixteen geomantic houses into sets of four (Mothers, Daughters, etc.) and their astrological strengths (Angular, Succeedent, and Cadent).</summary>

<keywords>The Inward Threshold, The Gathering, The Path, Ummahat (Mothers), Banat (Daughters), Avatad (Pillars), Panaphara (Succeedent), Apoklima (Cadent), Shudra, Vaishya</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical categorization of the geomantic houses, specifically defining their functional groupings and the "Witness" relationships between them.</meta>

# First Gem 1. ] ( 17 )

...are. ॥ 53 ॥ The first, fourth, seventh, and tenth houses (1, 4, 7, 10) are the centers <term>Kendra: angular houses that form the core of a chart</term>; these are also called "incarnations" <term>Avatar</term>. Houses 2, 5, 8, and 11 are "succeedent" houses <term>Panaphara</term> and are also known as *Mayal* <note>original: 'mayal', likely from the Arabic *mail* meaning inclined</note> by the learned. ॥ 54 ॥

Similarly, houses 3, 6, 9, and 12 are the "cadent" houses <term>Apoklima</term> and are also known as *Jayal* <note>original: 'jayal', likely from the Arabic *zail* meaning descending</note>. Now, the remaining places 13, 14, 15, and 16 are known by the names *Sadal* <note>original: 'sadal', perhaps related to the Arabic *sa'd* meaning fortunate</note> and *Avatar*. ॥ 55 ॥

> The eighth house is the witness for the thirteenth; for the body (the first house), the seventh; for the house of wealth (the second), the fourteenth. For the third house, the fifth and sixth; for the house of happiness (the fourth), the sixteenth. ॥ 56 ॥ For the house of the son (the fifth), the ninth; for the enemy’s house (the sixth), the tenth; for the partner’s house (the seventh), the eleventh; for the house of death (the eighth), the twelfth. For the ninth house, the fifteenth; for the tenth house, the eleventh; and the eighth is the witness for the twelfth house. ॥ 57 ॥ For the thirteenth house, the first; for the fourteenth house, the second; for the fifth, the ninth; for the fourth... the witness house for all houses is the fifteenth. ॥ 58 ॥

->Thus ends the First Gem on Definitions in the *Nine Gems of Ramal*. ॥ 1 ॥<-

**Commentary**—Now the "Witness Houses" <term>Sakshi-sthana: houses that provide additional testimony or confirmation for a primary house</term> are explained: 13-8, 1-7, 14-2, 6-5, 4-10, 5-9, 10-6, 7-11, 8-12, 9-13, 10-6, 11-7, 12-8, 13-9, 14-2, 15-9, 10, 4. This means that the witness house for the thirteenth is the eighth, for the first it is the seventh, and so on, as understood from the written numbers. In this manner, every house has its own witness house, and the fifteenth house is the witness station for all houses. ॥ 56-58 ॥

->Thus ends the first gem in the vernacular commentary on the *Nine Gems of Ramal* by Mahidhar. ॥ 1 ॥<-

## Opinion of the Vernacular Translator.

In the science of Ramal <term>Ramal Shastra: the art of geomancy practiced via patterns of dots</term>, eleven different sequences <note>original: 'krama'</note> are described for examining various types of matters. Because this is a small volume, only seven sequences are mentioned here. Even among those, the "Sequence of Omens" <note>original: 'shakun krama'</note>, which is the most universally useful, has been written. Although this sequence is the primary one, nonetheless—

<summary>This page defines the groupings of the sixteen houses into Angular (Kendra), Succeedent (Panaphara), and Cadent (Apoklima) categories, and details the "Witness House" relationships used to interpret geomantic charts.</summary>
<keywords>Kendra, Panaphara, Apoklima, Sakshi-sthana, Mahidhar, Ramal Navaratna, geomancy houses, witness houses, Shakun Krama</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues from the discussion of the "Witness Houses" in the First Gem. Having defined the basic house structures (Angular, Succeedent, Cadent), the author now transitions into the various "Sequences" or arrangements of geomantic figures used for specific types of divination.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>[ The Nine Gems of Ramal-</header>

The primary functions of other matters will be accomplished through these sequences in a way that they cannot be through any other. Therefore, drawing from the opinions of other treatises, I am recording their eleven sequences and their primary functions:—

( 1 ) **The Sequence of Omens** <term>Shakun-kram</term>॥ This is the natural state of success; all matters are first examined using this.

( 2 ) **The Sequence of Abdah** <term>Abdah-kram</term> <note>original: 'abdah', likely referring to a specific Arabic numbering or ordering system</note> ॥ This is especially useful for determining the nature of strength and for making decisions regarding offspring.

( 3 ) **The Sequence of Vijdah** <term>Vijdah-kram</term> ॥ This is used for indicating the time limit or duration <note>original: 'miyād'</note> of a task.

( 4 ) **The Sequence of Abjad** <term>Abjad-kram</term> <note>The 'Abjad' system assigns numerical values to the letters of the alphabet</note> ॥ Used for identifying the name of an object and its physical parts or limbs.

( 5 ) **The Sequence of Temperament** <term>Mijaj-kram</term> <note>original: 'mizāj', referring to the four humors or elemental qualities</note> ॥ Used for the successful completion of a task.

( 6 ) **The Sequence of Letters** <term>Harfi-kram</term> ॥ Used for considering the strength or weakness <note>original: 'balābal'</note> and the stability of the various sections.

( 7 ) **The Sequence of the Most Sound** <term>Asah-kram</term> ॥ Used for revealing things that have neither been heard nor seen.

( 8 ) **The Sequence of the Red** <term>Humra-kram</term> ॥ Used for obtaining commands and achieving success in tasks without requiring effort.

( 9 ) **The Sequence of Petitions** <term>Arj-kram</term> ॥ Used for profit without labor and for noble gains.

( 10 ) **The Sequence of the Return** <term>Ma'ad-kram</term> ॥ Used for success without effort and for achieving victory in battle.

( 11 ) **The Trine Sequence** <term>Musallas-kram</term> ॥ Used for making decisions regarding renunciation, disposal, and the completion of tasks.

These are the eleven sequences. For the convenience of the readers, their forms are written below in circular diagrams <term>Chakrakar</term>. Furthermore, the designations for the geomantic figures <note>original: 'shakal', the 16 patterns of dots used in Ramal</note> mentioned in the *Gem of Designations* <term>Sanjna-ratna</term> are also written below in circular diagrams for the readers' ease. Because this is a small book, the original author only mentioned a few primary designations; therefore, for the benefit of the readers, I am also writing more specific designations quoted from other larger, comprehensive texts:—

<summary>This page introduces eleven specialized sequences (kramas) of geomantic figures, each serving a distinct divinatory purpose such as identifying objects, predicting victory, or determining duration. The author notes that these will be presented in circular diagrams for clarity and expanded with information from larger treatises.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the physical characteristics of the sixteen geomantic figures (the "First Gem") to the "Second Gem," which focuses on the dignities or "strength" (balabala) of these figures. It establishes the elemental logic—Fire, Air, Water, and Earth—that determines whether a figure's prediction will actually manifest or remain "fruitless."</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>[ In the Nine Gems of Ramal— ]</header>

## Now, the Second Gem: On Strength and Weakness.

Figures that provide auspicious or inauspicious results do so according to their strength. Those with strength give their proper fruit; those without strength are known to be fruitless. Therefore, one must understand their relative strength and weakness. ॥ 1 ॥

Fire, Air, Water, and Earth elements appear in the sequence of the chart. Through the combination of these elements, the strength or weakness of the <term>figures</term> <note>Sanskrit: *khands*; the sixteen basic symbols of geomancy.</note> is determined. ॥ 2 ॥

Fire-element figures placed in Fire-element houses—and so on for the other elements—become strong. They are likewise strong when placed in the houses of their friends. ॥ 3 ॥

When situated in neutral houses, their strength remains average. In an enemy's house, there is a total lack of strength. Having understood this, one should pronounce the results [of the divination]. ॥ 4 ॥

Fire and Air are friends, as are Water and Earth. Fire and Water are enemies to each other. Likewise, Earth and Fire relate to others as neutrals. ॥ 5 ॥

This is the first method of determining the power of the figures based on their elements. The second method of strength is called the "Sequence of Seven Rows." ॥ 6 ॥

**Commentary**—Now, in this second "Gem," the <term>strength and weakness</term> <note>Sanskrit: *balabala*</note> of the figures is described. All figures give auspicious or inauspicious results according to whether they are powerful or weak. A strong figure certainly gives its full result, while a weak one is fruitless. Therefore, one must first consider the inquiry into strength and weakness. ॥ 1 ॥

It is like this: <term>Fire</term>, <term>Air</term>, <term>Water</term>, and <term>Earth</term>, which are sequentially called <term>Atashi</term>, <term>Baadi</term>, <term>Aabi</term>, and <term>Khaki</term> <note>The author uses Persian/Arabic technical terms common in Ramal: *Atashi* (Fire), *Baadi* (Air), *Aabi* (Water), and *Khaki* (Earth).</note>. Each house [in the geomantic chart] belongs to these elements in order. One must judge the strength through their combinations. ॥ 2 ॥

A Fire figure <insert>Fire</insert> in a Fire (*Atashi*) house, an Air figure in an Air house, and similarly Water in Water and Earth in Earth, are all strong. They are also strong in the houses of their friends. ॥ 3 ॥

In a neutral house, the strength is also neutral (average), and in an enemy’s house, it becomes weak. After knowing this, one should speak the fruit [the prediction]. ॥ 4 ॥

Fire and Air, as well as Water and Earth, are mutual friends...

<vocab>balabala (strength/weakness), Ramal (geomancy), khanda (figure), atashi (fiery), baadi (airy), aabi (watery), khaki (earthy), prastara (chart/layout), pankti (row/line)</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the elemental theory of geomancy, explaining that the sixteen figures only provide results if they are "strong" based on their placement. It outlines a system of elemental friendships (e.g., Fire and Air) and enmities (Fire and Water) used to judge the potency of a divination.</summary>
<keywords>geomancy, Ramal, elemental dignities, five elements, Atashi, Baadi, Aabi, Khaki, divination strength, Sanskrit manuscript</keywords>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>[Second Gem, Part 2]</header>

| The Cycle of Friends and Foes | | | | |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| — | Fire (A) | Air (Va) | Water (Ja) | Earth (Bhu) |
| **Neutral** | Earth | Water | Air | Fire |
| **Enemy** | Water | Earth | Fire | Air |
| **Friend** | Air | Fire | Earth | Water |

Water and Fire are enemies, as are Earth and Fire; others are neutral. This is visible in the chart above. ॥ 5 ॥ In this way, the first determination of <term>strength and weakness</term> <note>Sanskrit: *balabala*</note> is based on the elemental nature of the figures. The second method of determining strength is called the "Sequence of the Seven Rows." ॥ 6 ॥

---

> I shall speak of the arrangement of the seven activities: <term>Shakuna</term>, <term>Abdah</term>, <term>Vizdah</term>, <term>Abjad</term>, <term>Mizaj</term>, <term>Harfa</term>, and <term>Asah</term>. ॥ 7 ॥ <note>These seven names refer to different systems of ordering the sixteen geomantic figures, many of which are derived from Persian and Arabic divination traditions (Ramal).</note>
> 
> The <term>Shakuna</term> sequence is specified first because it is inherently useful for all purposes without a specific cause. Now, I shall speak of the logic for establishing the <term>Abdah</term> sequence. ॥ 8 ॥

**Commentary**—The establishment of these seven sequences—1. Shakuna, 2. Abdah, 3. Vizdah, 4. Abjad, 5. Mizaj, 6. Harfa, and 7. Asah—is now explained. ॥ 7 ॥ First, the Shakuna sequence is used for everyone’s benefit without needing a special reason, and it is the most important for all tasks. Other sequences are primary for their own specific purposes. At this time, we provide the reasoning for the establishment of the **Abdah sequence**. ॥ 8 ॥

---

> Within each <term>figure</term> <note>Sanskrit: *khanda*, referring to the four-tiered geomantic symbols.</note>, the four elements—Fire and the rest—are situated. Their order is from top to bottom; they are known as "manifest" or "hidden" based on the presence of dots or lines. ॥ 9 ॥
> 
> In the Abdah sequence, there are four letters with the numerical values of 1, 2, 4, and 8. By totaling the values of the manifest elements, one determines the position of that figure in the Abdah sequence. ॥ 10 ॥

**Commentary**—The four elements, starting with Fire, are present in every figure. In the vertical order from top to bottom, where there is a <term>dot</term> <note>Sanskrit: *bindu*</note>, the element is "manifest" (active), and where there is a <term>line</term> <note>Sanskrit: *rekha*</note>, the element is "hidden" (inactive). ॥ 9 ॥ In the **Abdah sequence**, there are four letters representing the four tiers of a figure: 'A' equals 1, 'B' equals 2, 'D' equals 4, and 'H' equals 8. <note>The name "Abdah" comes from the Hebrew/Arabic letters Alif (A), Ba (B), Dal (D), and Ha (H).</note> By adding the values of only the manifest elements (the dots), the resulting sum determines that figure's placement in the sequence...

<summary>This page explains the "Cycle of Friends and Foes" among the elements and introduces the seven systems of sequencing geomantic figures, focusing on the Abdah system which assigns mathematical values (1, 2, 4, 8) to the elemental tiers.</summary>
<keywords>Abdah, elements, Fire, Air, Water, Earth, Shakuna, manifest and hidden, dots and lines, geomancy, Ramal, numerical values</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>The text transitions from defining the seven systems of ordering geomantic figures to the specific mathematical logic of the "Abdah" (A-B-D-H) sequence. This page explains how the elemental composition of each figure determines its numerical position within the 16 houses of a geomantic chart, specifically for the Abdah and Vizdah systems.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>[ In the Ramala-Navaratna — The Nine Gems of Ramala ]</header>

The status is to be known by this; thus is the **A-B-D-H** sequence. ॥ 10 ॥

Just as in the figure <term>Lahyan</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Lihyan*, meaning "The Beard." Associated with the planet Jupiter.</note>, there is a union with the form of the **Fire element**. By that number in the **Abdah** sequence, the position of *Lahyan* is established. ॥ 11 ॥

In the figure <term>Tarikh</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Tariq*, meaning "The Road."</note>, there is the manifestation of all four elements. These are born of the letters of **Abdah**, and when their numbers are combined, their sum establishes the position of *Tarikh* in the sequence. ॥ 12 ॥

Where no element is manifested, that number is not obtained. The remaining figure, <term>Jamat</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Jama'at*, meaning "The Gathering" or "The Crowd."</note>, is placed in the sixteenth house. ॥ 13 ॥

For determining the <term>appearance of a thief</term>, or for matters concerning <term>pregnancy and progeny</term>, the **Abdah** system should be used. In the same way, the positions of the figures in the **Vizdah** sequence are to be understood. ॥ 14 ॥

**Commentary**—In the figure *Lahyan*, the Fire element is "revealed" (represented by a single dot) at the top. In the **Abdah** system, the first letter **A** (Alif) equals 1. Since *Lahyan* only has this single point at the top, its total value is 1, and thus it occupies the first house in this sequence. <note>geomantic symbol for Lahyan: ∴ (one dot over two lines)</note> ॥ 11 ॥ 

Furthermore, in the figure *Tarikh*, all four elements are revealed <note>geomantic symbol for Tarikh: ⁝ (four dots arranged vertically)</note>. Thus, we add the values of all the letters: **A** (1) + **B** (2) + **D** (4) + **H** (8). The sum of these is 15. For this reason, the figure *Tarikh* is assigned to the fifteenth house in this sequence. ॥ 12 ॥ 

Where no element is revealed (represented by a line instead of a dot), that specific number is not added. Consequently, the figure *Jamat* <note>geomantic symbol for Jamat: ☷ (four horizontal lines, no dots)</note> finds its place in the sixteenth house. Because the maximum sum of the letters **A-B-D-H** only reaches 15, *Jamat* (having no dots/numerical value) is moved to the 16th house by default. This is the logic of the **Abdah** sequence. ॥ 13 ॥ 

This specific sequence is calculated when one is trying to visualize the form of a thief or considering questions about children and pregnancy. The positions of the figures in the **Vizdah** sequence should be understood in a similar fashion. ॥ 14 ॥

In the **Vizdah** system, the letter **Ja** (Jim) is assigned the number seven. The remaining letters are derived from the **Abdah** values. Just as before, the position of the figures in the **Vizdah** sequence is determined by the sum of these values. ॥ 15 ॥

<summary>This page explains the mathematical assignment of geomantic figures to specific houses based on their elemental dots, focusing on the Abdah system (1, 2, 4, 8) and its use in predicting pregnancy or identifying thieves.</summary>
<keywords>Abdah system, Lahyan, Tarikh, Jamat, Vizdah system, geomancy calculation, divination for thieves, pregnancy divination</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how geomantic figures are assigned to specific houses (1–16) based on their elemental composition. Having previously discussed the "Abdah" sequence, the text now details the mathematical logic of the "Vijdah" system, where letters are assigned different numerical values to determine a figure's position.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>[ The Second Gem, Part 2 ]</header>

# Just as the figure <term>Lahyan</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Lihyan*, associated with the planet Jupiter and the Fire element.</note> contains the Fire element, it is associated with the number two. By this union,
# the position of *Lahyan* is established in the second house according to the **Vijdah** sequence. ॥ 16 ॥
# The figure <term>Farha</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Farah*, meaning "Joy." Associated with Venus.</note> is formed by the sum of seventeen; when sixteen is subtracted by the rule of the houses,
# the position of *Farha* is known to be in the first house. In the figure <term>Tarikh</term>, all elements are manifest. ॥ 17 ॥
# When the total sum is reduced by sixteen, the remainder is five, establishing the position of *Tarikh* in the fifth house. Such is the numerical science of the <term>Abad</term> <note>The *Abjad* or alphanumerical system where letters represent numbers.</note> and its other purposes. ॥ 18 ॥

**Commentary—** In the **Vijdah** sequence, the letter *Ja* (J) carries the value of 7, while the others are as before: *Va* (V) is 2, *Da* (D) is 4, and *Ha* (H) is 8. Just as with the previous *Abdah* system, the positions within the houses are determined by adding these numerical values. ॥ 15 ॥ For example, in the figure *Lahyan*, the Fire element is represented by a single dot. Since the letter *Va* (representing Fire) equals 2, *Lahyan* is placed in the second house. ॥ 16 ॥ In the figure *Farha*, three elements are manifest: Fire, Air, and Earth. We take the values of *Va* (2), *Ja* (7), and *Ha* (8). Their sum is 17. Because this exceeds the 16 houses of the geomantic chart, we subtract 16, leaving a remainder of 1. Therefore, *Farha* is positioned in the first house. In the figure *Tarikh*, all four elements are present, so we add all the values: *Va* (2), *Ja* (7), *Da* (4), and *Ha* (8). The sum is 21. Subtracting 16 leaves a remainder of 5, placing *Tarikh* in the fifth house. This describes the numerical order; it has further applications as well. ॥ 17 ॥ ॥ 18 ॥

->Now, the definitions of the **Abdah** and **Vijdah** sequences.<-

# <term>Lahyan</term>, <term>Humra</term> <note>The Red; Mars.</note>, then <term>Nusratu’l Kharij</term> <note>Outward Victory.</note>, <term>Kabzu’l Kharij</term> <note>Outward Capture.</note>, <term>Ijtima</term> <note>The Gathering.</note>, 
# <term>Utba-i-Kharij</term> <note>The Outward Threshold.</note>, <term>Ankis</term> <note>The Inverted.</note>, <term>Uklah</term> <note>The Nut/Morsel.</note>, 
# <term>Kabzu’l Dakhil</term> <note>Inward Capture.</note>, <term>Farha</term>, <term>Nusratu’l Dakhil</term> <note>Inward Victory.</note>, 
# <term>Naqi</term> <note>The Pure.</note>, then <term>Utba-i-Dakhil</term> <note>The Inward Threshold.</note>, <term>Tarikh</term>, and <term>Jamat</term> make sixteen in the **Abdah** system. ॥ 19 ॥ 
# In the **Vijdah** system, they are: <term>Farha</term>, <term>Lahyan</term>, <term>Utba-i-Dakhil</term>...

<header>[ In the Nine Gems of Ramal - ]</header>

...the figure <term>Kabzul-Kharij</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Qabd al-Kharij*, "The Outward Grip." Associated with Saturn.</note> is established. The figures <term>Humra</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Hamra*, "The Red." Associated with Mars.</note>, <term>Ankish</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Inqis*, "The Inverted." Associated with Saturn.</note>, and <term>Nakha</term> are likewise declared. ॥ 20 ॥
The figures <term>Jitam</term>, <term>Anada</term>, <term>Atavakh</term>, and <term>Naki</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Naqi*, "The Pure." Associated with the Sun.</note> follow. These sixteen divisions are thus proclaimed within this **Vijdah** sequence. ॥ 21 ॥

**Commentary**—In these verses (19, 20, and 21), the sequences for **Abdah** and **Vijdah** are explained. Their clear meanings, along with their numerical forms, are illustrated in the previously mentioned chart. Readers should refer there for clarity. ॥ 19 ॥ 20 ॥ 21 ॥

### Now, the Definition of the Abjad Sequence.

From the numbers one, two, three, and four <note>Sanskrit: *Rupa* (1), *Akshi* (2), *Rama* (3), and *Abdhi* (4).</note>, the origin of the ten <term>Abjad</term> houses arises in this order. These are established from the houses of the omens, placed before or after, or from the first and last houses of the horoscope. ॥ 22 ॥
In this system, the ten numbers determine the position of the figure <term>Tarikh</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Tariq*, "The Path." Associated with the Moon.</note> in the "sky" <note>The 10th house, representing the zenith or midheaven.</note>. The figure <term>Jamatam</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Jama'at*, "The Congregation." Associated with the Moon.</note> is designated by the <term>Yavanas</term> <note>Literally "Greeks," but here referring to the Perso-Arabic practitioners of the science of *Ramal* (Geomancy).</note> for the King. The figures <term>Lahyan</term>, <term>Humra</term>, <term>Vayaj</term>, <term>Nakish</term>, and the one called <term>Kabzul-Dakhil</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Qabd al-Dakhil*, "The Inward Grip." Associated with Jupiter.</note> are also fully described. ॥ 23 ॥
The figures <term>Farha</term>, <term>Naki</term>, <term>Atavadam</term>, <term>Tarikh</term>, <term>Nusrut</term>, <term>Kabzul-Kharij</term>, and <term>Istima</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Ijtima*, "The Conjunction." Associated with the Moon.</note> are included. <term>Atavekh</term>, <term>Nusrut-Dakhil</term>, and <term>Jamatam</term>—these are to be arranged according to these specific letter-numbers. ॥ 24 ॥

**Commentary**—The definition of the **Abjad** sequence is as follows: The letters A (1), B (2), J (3), and D (4) are the numerical values. Since their total sum is 10, they are written in order across ten planetary positions according to these digits. Beyond this, up to the 16th house, the figures are established by looking at the preceding and succeeding omens. ॥ 22 ॥ Because there are 10 numbers, the position of <term>Tarikh</term> is in the 10th house. Beyond this, one must count by casting out nines <note>A common mathematical reduction method in Indian and Arabic numerology.</note> and starting again from one. For the 11th house, the number 3 is used; after casting out nine, the figure <term>Bayaz</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Bayad*, "The White." Associated with Venus.</note> is placed in the 10th house. Here, the number 3 also represents the figure <term>Nusrut-Khalij</term>, but since <term>Bayaz</term> appears first in the sequence of omens, <term>Bayaz</term> is the one assigned to the 3rd position...

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>The Second Gem 2. ]</header>

In this order, all sixteen houses should be understood as they were spoken of by the <term>Yavanas</term> <note>Sanskrit: *Yavana*. Originally meaning "Ionian/Greek," in this context it refers to the Perso-Arabic practitioners of Ramal geomancy.</note> in the <term>Jama'at</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Jama'a*, meaning "The Gathering" or "The Group."</note> sequence. These are: **Lahyan**, **Humra**, **Bayaj**, **Ankish**, **Uqba**, and **Kanzul-dakhil**. ॥ 23 ॥
**Farha**, **Naki**, **Atave-dakhil**, **Tarikh**, (10) **Nusrat-kharij**, **Kanzul-kharij**, **Istima**, **Atave-kharij**, **Nusrud-dakhil**, and **Jama'at**. These sixteen names should be known in this specific order. ॥ 24 ॥

->**Now, the Sequence of the Balance.**<-

Starting from the Sun, every sixth planet <note>Sanskrit: *shashtha-shashtha-grahanam*. A calculation where one counts every six positions in the planetary order.</note> in succession establishes the sequence of the <term>Shakuna</term> <note>Omens or geomantic figures.</note> starting from the first and ending with the portion of **Rahu** <note>The North Node of the Moon.</note>. Once again, following the same method starting from the ninth house, one should write them down until the portion of **Ketu** <note>The South Node of the Moon.</note> is reached; this is known as the **Mizaj** <note>Arabic/Persian: "Temperament."</note> or **Mizan** <note>Arabic: "Balance."</note> sequence. ॥ 25 ॥
The order is: **Kanju-dakhil**, **Farha**, **Jama'at**, **Vayaj**, **Uqba**, and **Lahyan**. Then **Humra**, **Kanzul-kharij**, and **Nusrat-kharij**, followed by **Atave-dakhil**. ॥ 26 ॥
Then **Istiya**, **Tarikh**, **Ankish**, **Nusrud-dakhil**, **Naki**, and finally **Atave-kharij**. This sequence has another purpose: it is to be used for determining the day of the week and the proper timing for performing various tasks. ॥ 27 ॥

**Commentary**—In the **Mizaj** and **Mizan** sequence, the figures are established according to the order of every sixth planet starting from the Sun. In the first set, the "Omen" <note>Hindi: *Shakun*. Referring to the first figure in the sequence.</note> is at the beginning and the portion of Rahu is at the end. These comprise the first eight positions. Following this, from the ninth position upward, the next eight are written in the same manner, ending with the portion of Ketu. This is called the **Mizaj** sequence. ॥ 25 ॥
Their arrangement is as follows: 1. **Kanzul-dakhil** <note>Arabic: *al-Qabd al-Dakhil*, "The Inward Grip."</note>, 2. **Farha** <note>Arabic: *al-Farah*, "Joy."</note>, 3. **Jama'at** <note>Arabic: *al-Jama'a*, "The Gathering."</note>, 4. **Vayaj** <note>Arabic: *al-Bayad*, "The White."</note>, 5. **Uqba** <note>Arabic: *al-Uqla*, "The Knot."</note>, 6. **Lahyan** <note>Arabic: *al-Lihyan*, "The Beard."</note>, 7. **Humra** <note>Arabic: *al-Hamra*, "The Red."</note>, 8. **Kanzul-kharij** <note>Arabic: *al-Qabd al-Kharij*, "The Outward Grip."</note>, 9. **Nusrat-kharij** <note>Arabic: *al-Nasr al-Kharij*, "Outward Victory."</note>, 10. **Atave-dakhil** <note>Arabic: *Ataba al-Dakhila*, "The Threshold Inward."</note>. ॥ 26 ॥
11. **Istiya** <note>Arabic: *al-Ijtima*, "The Conjunction."</note>, 12. **Tarikh** <note>Arabic: *al-Tariq*, "The Path."</note>, 13. **Ankish** <note>Arabic: *al-Inqis*, "The Inverted."</note>, 14. **Nusrud-dakhil** <note>Arabic: *al-Nasr al-Dakhil*, "Inward Victory."</note>, 15. **Naki** <note>Arabic: *al-Naqi*, "The Pure."</note>, 16. **Atave-kharij** <note>Arabic: *Ataba al-Kharija*, "The Threshold Outward."</note>. The purpose of this sixteen-fold sequence is to identify the day of the week and the appropriate time for success in one's endeavors. ॥ 27 ॥
